Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, thank you for seeing me. I wasn't even sure I should come in today, to be honest. I was sitting at the kitchen table this morning, just looking out the window with my coffee getting cold, and I thought, well, maybe I should just cancel. I don't like to be a bother to anyone, you know? I've never been one to run to the doctor for every little thing. I've always just pushed through. But... well, things have just been feeling so heavy lately. \n\nI've just been in this sort of rut, I guess you could call it. Just sitting in my chair, not really doing much of anything, watching the clock. But the real reason, the thing that finally made me keep this appointment... what really scared me enough to come in, is my thoughts lately. I just keep thinking... well, I keep thinking it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. \n\nIt's just this overwhelming feeling, like everything is just too much to carry anymore. Now, I want to be very clear, I haven't tried to hurt myself or anything like that. I don't have a plan to do anything drastic, I really don't. I wouldn't do that. But just... lying there in the mornings, realizing I have to face another whole day, and just wishing, hoping almost, that I could just slip away quietly in my sleep... it frightened me to realize I was thinking that way so often. It's not like me to think like that at all. I used to be so busy, always doing something, but now my mind just goes straight to that dark place, and I sit there and wonder, why am I thinking this? Is it normal to just want to be done with it all? It just really shook me up to realize I meant it, that I really just wanted to not wake up. So, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things anymore, or that activities you used to enjoy don’t seem to interest you?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Yes, that's exactly it, doctor. It's like you're reading my mind, because I used to have this whole routine, you see. I've always been one to keep busy. I have this little corner set up in the sunroom where I do my watercolors. I've been doing them for years—mostly little landscapes and birds, nothing professional, just for me, but I used to wake up and really look forward to the morning light hitting that room so I could sit and paint. \n\nAnd then on Tuesdays, I had my dance class down at the community center. Oh, we had such a good time, just moving and laughing to the music. And I'd go walking with the ladies in the neighborhood, too. We had a little group, three or four of us depending on the day, and we'd walk the whole loop around the park, rain or shine, just talking about our families and the neighborhood. \n\nBut lately... I just don't know what's happened to me. I just leave my painting things sitting right there on the table. The water cup is bone dry and the brushes are probably getting stiff because I didn't even put them away properly the last time I touched them. The ladies will call and ask if I'm coming for our walk, or if I'm going to the dance class, and I just... I make up an excuse. I tell them my knee is acting up or I'm waiting on a phone call. But the truth is, I just can't make myself care. \n\nI look at my paints, or I look at my walking shoes sitting right there by the front door, and I just feel... nothing. It’s like the color has just drained out of everything. I used to love those things so much, I really did. They were the highlight of my week. The girls called me just this past Thursday—or was it Wednesday? No, it was Thursday, because it was trash day—and they were going to do the big loop around the duck pond, and I just stood there holding the phone and thought, what's the point? I just couldn't muster up a single ounce of interest to go out that door. It's such a strange feeling, to look at the things you used to love and just feel completely empty about them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately—like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, my sleep... no, I wouldn't say it's changed, really. I mean, I still go to bed at the same time I always have, right after the ten o'clock news. I've always been very particular about that, ever since my husband was working the early shift at the plant. We'd watch the weather report, maybe a little bit of the late show, and then turn in. And I still do that, even now. I get into bed, I turn off the lamp, and I sleep through the night just fine. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in a panic or anything like that. \n\nAnd in the morning, I wake up at my usual time, right around six-thirty when the sun starts coming through the blinds in the bedroom. So no, I wouldn't say I'm sleeping more than usual, or having trouble falling asleep. The sleeping part itself is perfectly normal. It's just... like I was saying before, it's what happens *after* I wake up that's the problem. I wake up, and I just lie there, looking at the ceiling, thinking about how heavy the day feels before it's even started. But the actual sleeping? No, that hasn't changed a bit. I suppose I should be thankful for that, at least, since I hear so many people my age complaining about insomnia and having to take those little pills to get any rest. But for me, the sleep itself is just the same as it's always been.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I would say that's exactly right. Most of the day, nearly every day. It's just... well, it starts right when I wake up. Like I said about waking up at six-thirty, when the sun comes through the blinds? Well, I open my eyes, and almost immediately, before I even sit up or put my feet on the floor, there's just this... this awful, heavy sadness right here in my chest. \n\nIt feels almost like a physical weight, like someone set a cinder block right on top of my ribs while I was sleeping. And I just lie there under the covers, and I feel like I've already failed the day before it even starts. It's the strangest thing. I haven't done anything yet, I haven't even made my coffee or brushed my teeth, and already I feel this overwhelming sense of... of defeat, I suppose. \n\nAnd it just stays with me. It doesn't lift. I used to be able to shake off a bad mood, you know? If I woke up on the wrong side of the bed, I'd go make myself a nice breakfast, maybe put on some music—I love the old Motown records, they always used to get my toes tapping—and by the time I was washing the dishes, I'd be feeling right as rain. But not anymore. Now, I carry that heavy sadness around with me all day long. \n\nI'll be sitting in my chair in the living room at two in the afternoon, looking out at the street, watching the neighbor's kids riding their bikes up and down the sidewalk, and I just feel so incredibly down. It's like a dark cloud that just follows me from room to room, from the minute I wake up with that heavy feeling in my chest until I finally go back to sleep. So yes, doctor, to answer your question, it really is most of the day, and it feels like it's been every single day for a while now. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even when you haven't done much of anything?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. It's exactly like you said—even when I haven't done a single blessed thing. It goes right back to what I was just telling you about my sleep, you know? Because I get a full night in bed, I really do. I sleep all the way through from the news until the sun comes up, but... well, when I finally do push the covers off and try to get moving, my body just feels completely worn out. It makes no sense to me, because I haven't done anything to be tired from! \n\nI used to have energy to spare, but now... well, just take the laundry, for example. I have my washer and dryer right there in a little closet off the kitchen, so it's not like I'm lugging baskets up and down two flights of steep basement stairs like we had to do in our old house on Elm Street. But just picking up that little plastic laundry basket—the white one with the blue handles—and carrying it those few steps into the kitchen... it just takes so much more out of me than it ever should. I'll set it down and have to lean against the counter or sit at the kitchen table just to catch my breath. \n\nAnd breakfast! Making breakfast used to be just second nature. I've made breakfast every day of my adult life. I'd whip up some eggs, maybe toast a couple slices of rye bread, get the coffee perking... but now, just standing at the stove or trying to put a simple meal together, it just absolutely exhausts me. I have to drag my feet across the linoleum. I feel like I've been doing hard labor before I've even poured my morning coffee. It's just this bone-deep tiredness, doctor, where every little movement feels like wading through molasses, and it stays with me all day long, even when I'm just sitting in my chair doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. My mind is actually... well, it's sharp as a tack, really. I mean, my body is exhausted, like I was just telling you with the laundry and the breakfast and all that, but my head? No, my thinking is just fine. \n\nI still do my word searches every single afternoon. I sit in my chair by the front window—the one with the blue floral pattern, I've had it since about 1998, bought it at Sears back when they were still at the mall—and I can sit there and find every single word on the page without losing my place or getting distracted. And making decisions? I don't have too many big decisions to make these days, being on my own and not working, but I still handle all my own affairs perfectly well. I pay all my bills on time, I balance my checkbook down to the penny every Tuesday morning. I sit right at the kitchen table with my calculator, the one with the big numbers so I don't have to squint, and I go through all the receipts from the grocery store. Forgetting where they put their pocketbooks or losing the plot of their television shows. I know exactly what's going on. I might be terribly sad, and I might be tired down to my bones, but my thinking is as clear as it's ever been. It's just... I just don't have the energy to actually *do* the things I'm thinking about, if you see what I mean. But the focusing part itself, no, I haven't had any trouble with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, doctor, no. I really don't have any trouble with my thinking or my concentration at all. It's a bit funny you ask it again, because I know I just went on and on about my checkbook and my word searches, but it really is the truth! My mind is perfectly fine. \n\nI can sit down with the Sunday paper—you know, the big thick one that gets delivered by that nice boy from down the street, Tommy, I think his name is, though he must be getting ready to graduate high school soon because he's gotten so tall—anyway, I can sit with that paper and read every single article on the front page, right through to the end, without losing my train of thought even once. Even the complicated articles about the city council meetings and the zoning laws for that new shopping plaza they want to build over on Route 9. I read all of it, and I understand it just fine. \n\nI don't have to go back and re-read paragraphs like some folks do when their minds start to wander. My mind doesn't wander at all. It just... stays put, right where it's supposed to be. So, no, whether it's simple tasks like I mentioned before with the calculator, or harder ones like following the local politics in the paper, my concentration is just as good as it was thirty years ago. It's just the awful tiredness and that heavy sadness, like I told you. But the thinking part? No, I haven't found that hard at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite has been just about the same as it always is. I mean, I know I was telling you earlier about how exhausting it is just to stand at the stove and make breakfast, but I still eat it. I sit right down at that kitchen table—the one with the little yellow checkered tablecloth I bought at Kmart years ago—and I eat my eggs and my rye toast. \n\nI haven't noticed my clothes fitting any differently, either. I still wear the same slacks I've been wearing for the last five years, those comfortable ones with the elastic waistband. They aren't falling off me, and they aren't pinching me at the waist, so I must be staying right around the same weight. I don't step on the scale very often, to be perfectly honest with you. I have one of those old dial scales in the bathroom, tucked right between the sink and the bathtub, but half the time I forget it's even there. \n\nBut no, I make sure I eat my three meals a day. I might just have a sandwich for lunch—usually turkey with a little bit of mayonnaise on white bread, maybe a pickle on the side if I have a jar open in the fridge—and then something simple for dinner. I don't have the energy to cook big elaborate meals like I used to when the house was full, like roasting a whole chicken or making a big pot of beef stew that takes hours to simmer, but I still eat a normal amount. So no, my appetite and my weight, they've been perfectly steady. No changes there at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore—not even things you used to enjoy, like your watercolors or music?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. It's funny you mention the watercolors, because I was just looking at them the other day. I have this little setup in the spare room—the one that used to be my daughter's room before she moved out to Ohio, it gets that wonderful morning light through the east-facing window. I've had my easel set up there for, oh, it must be five or six years now. I used to go to this senior dance class at the community center on Wednesday evenings. I haven't done anything yet, I haven't even made my coffee or brushed my teeth, and already I feel this overwhelming sense of... of defeat, I suppose. \n\nAnd it just stays with me. It doesn't lift. If I woke up on the wrong side of the bed, I'd go make myself a nice breakfast, maybe put on some music—I love the old Motown records, they always used to get my toes tapping—and by the time I was washing the dishes, I'd be feeling right as rain. But not anymore. Now, I carry that heavy sadness around with me all day long. \n\nI'll be sitting in my chair in the living room at two in the afternoon, looking out at the street, watching the neighbor's kids riding their bikes up and down the sidewalk, and I just feel so incredibly down. It's like a dark cloud that just follows me from room to room, from the minute I wake up with that heavy feeling in my chest until I finally go back to sleep. So yes, doctor, to answer your question, it really is most of the day, and it feels like it's been every single day for a while now. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even when you haven't done much of anything?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. It's exactly like you said—even when I haven't done a single blessed thing. It goes right back to what I was just telling you about my sleep, you know? Because I get a full night in bed, I really do. I sleep all the way through from the news until the sun comes up, but... well, when I finally do push the covers off and try to get moving, my body just feels completely worn out. It makes no sense to me, because I haven't done anything to be tired from! \n\nI used to have energy to spare, but now... well, just take the laundry, for example. I have my washer and dryer right there in a little closet off the kitchen, so it's not like I'm lugging baskets up and down two flights of steep basement stairs like we had to do in our old house on Elm Street. But just picking up that little plastic laundry basket—the white one with the blue handles—and carrying it those few steps into the kitchen... it just takes so much more out of me than it ever should. I'll set it down and have to lean against the counter or sit at the kitchen table just to catch my breath. \n\nAnd breakfast! Making breakfast used to be just second nature. I've made breakfast every day of my adult life. I'd whip up some eggs, maybe toast a couple slices of rye bread, get the coffee perking... but now, just standing at the stove or trying to put a simple meal together, it just absolutely exhausts me. I have to drag my feet across the linoleum. I feel like I've been doing hard labor before I've even poured my morning coffee. It's just this bone-deep tiredness, doctor, where every little movement feels like wading through molasses, and it stays with me all day long, even when I'm just sitting in my chair doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. My mind is actually... well, it's sharp as a tack, really. I mean, my body is exhausted, like I was just telling you with the laundry and the breakfast and all that, but my head? No, my thinking is just fine. \n\nI still do my word searches every single afternoon. I sit in my chair by the front window—the one with the blue floral pattern, I've had it since about 1998, bought it at Sears back when they were still at the mall—and I can sit there and find every single word on the page without losing my place or getting distracted. And making decisions? I don't have too many big decisions to make these days, being on my own and not working, but I still handle all my own affairs perfectly well. I pay all my bills on time, I balance my checkbook down to the penny every Tuesday morning. I sit right at the kitchen table with my calculator, the one with the big numbers so I don't have to squint, and I go through all the receipts from the grocery store. I don't get confused at all. \n\nI know some ladies my age start getting a little... well, a little foggy, you know? Forgetting where they put their pocketbooks or losing the plot of their television shows. My friend Martha, bless her heart, she can't even follow Jeopardy! anymore because it moves too fast for her, and she gets all turned around. But me? No, I can follow along just fine. I know exactly what's going on. I might be terribly sad, and I might be tired down to my bones, but my thinking is as clear as it's ever been. It's just... I just don't have the energy to actually *do* the things I'm thinking about, if you see what I mean. But the focusing part itself, no, I haven't had any trouble with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, doctor, no. I really don't have any trouble with my thinking or my concentration at all. It's a bit funny you ask it again, because I know I just went on and on about my checkbook and my word searches, but it really is the truth! My mind is perfectly fine. \n\nI can sit down with the Sunday paper—you know, the big thick one that gets delivered by that nice boy from down the street, Tommy, I think his name is, though he must be getting ready to graduate high school soon because he's gotten so tall—anyway, I can sit with that paper and read every single article on the front page, right through to the end, without losing my train of thought even once. Even the complicated articles about the city council meetings and the zoning laws for that new shopping plaza they want to build over on Route 9. I read all of it, and I understand it just fine. \n\nI don't have to go back and re-read paragraphs like some folks do when their minds start to wander. My mind doesn't wander at all. It just... stays put, right where it's supposed to be. So, no, whether it's simple tasks like I mentioned before with the calculator, or harder ones like following the local politics in the paper, my concentration is just as good as it was thirty years ago. It's just the awful tiredness and that heavy sadness, like I told you. But the thinking part? No, I haven't found that hard at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite has been just about the same as it always is. I mean, I know I was telling you earlier about how exhausting it is just to stand at the stove and make breakfast, but I still eat it. I sit right down at that kitchen table—the one with the little yellow checkered tablecloth I bought at Kmart years ago—and I eat my eggs and my rye toast. \n\nI haven't noticed my clothes fitting any differently, either. I still wear the same slacks I've been wearing for the last five years, those comfortable ones with the elastic waistband. They aren't falling off me, and they aren't pinching me at the waist, so I must be staying right around the same weight. I don't step on the scale very often, to be perfectly honest with you. I have one of those old dial scales in the bathroom, tucked right between the sink and the bathtub, but half the time I forget it's even there. \n\nBut no, I make sure I eat my three meals a day. I might just have a sandwich for lunch—usually turkey with a little bit of mayonnaise on white bread, maybe a pickle on the side if I have a jar open in the fridge—and then something simple for dinner. I don't have the energy to cook big elaborate meals like I used to when the house was full, like roasting a whole chicken or making a big pot of beef stew that takes hours to simmer, but I still eat a normal amount. So no, my appetite and my weight, they've been perfectly steady. No changes there at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore—not even things you used to enjoy, like your watercolors or music?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. It's funny you mention the watercolors, because I was just looking at them the other day. I have this little setup in the spare room—the one that used to be my daughter's room before she moved out to Ohio, it gets that wonderful morning light through the east-facing window. I've had my easel set up there for, oh, it must be five or six years now. I used to go in there every Tuesday and Thursday morning after breakfast, mix my paints on that little plastic palette I bought at the craft store over on Main Street, and just paint for hours. Flowers, mostly. Irises were my favorite. \n\nBut lately? I walk past that room, I look at the easel, I see the paints just sitting there getting dusty, and I just... I don't care. I can't make myself care. The feeling I used to get, that little spark of excitement when I'd get the color just right? It's completely gone. And it's not just the painting, either. I used to go to this senior dance class at the community center on Wednesday evenings. We'd do a little line dancing, some gentle ballroom steps—nothing too strenuous, just nice music and good company. I haven't been in weeks. The thought of putting on my shoes and driving over there just seems so pointless now. \n\nEven my morning walks with the ladies... I used to meet Shirley and Barbara at the park entrance right at 8:00 AM sharp. We'd walk the perimeter path, the one that goes around the duck pond, and chat about everything under the sun. Now, when the phone rings and I know it's Shirley calling to see if I'm coming, I just let it ring. I sit in my chair and listen to it ring, and I don't feel a single thing. I just leave all my things sitting there—my walking shoes by the door, my paints in the spare room—and I just can't find a single reason to pick them up. It's like all the color has just drained right out of everything I used to love.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—like nothing brings you pleasure and you're carrying this heavy sadness most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I'd say it's been going on for about four months. Yes, four months exactly, because it started right around the time my aunt came to live with us. She moved into the downstairs bedroom—the one we used to use as a den, where my husband had his old recliner and the television set we bought when they finally switched everything over to digital. We had to move all of his things out into the garage to make room for her bed and her dresser. \n\nShe's my mother's sister, Aunt Clara, and she's getting up there in years, you know, she just turned eighty-seven in October. She couldn't manage the stairs in her old house anymore, the one over on Elm Street with the big wrap-around porch. Beautiful house, but just too much for her to keep up with, especially after she had that little fall in the kitchen. So, we brought her here, and ever since then... well, that's when this whole heavy feeling really settled in. \n\nI remember it was just after we got her all unpacked. I had put her favorite floral quilt on the bed, and I just sat down in the living room and felt this awful, crushing weight come over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. Not one bit. Every single day for the last four months, I wake up and that heavy sadness is just sitting right there on my chest, before I even put my feet on the floor. It's been four long months of just... nothing. No joy in anything at all, just like I was saying about my paints and my walks. Just four months of this awful, heavy grayness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my husband, Richard—he's always been the observant one, you know, notices every little thing, like if I move a vase two inches to the left on the mantle—he's been saying something about that. He tells me I'm moving like everything takes extra effort now. Like I'm wading through molasses or something. And I suppose he's right. I just feel so heavy. Like when I go to get up from the sofa—the blue one we bought at Sears years ago, the one with the little matching throw pillows—it just takes me so much longer to actually get to my feet. I have to think about it, push myself up, and it just feels exhausting. 

And it's not just around the house, either. Even when I manage to get myself to the grocery store—the Stop & Shop over on County Road, I've been going there since they opened in '98—I'll just be standing there in the aisle. I went in last week just to get a carton of milk and some of those little oyster crackers for Aunt Clara's soup. And I just stood there in front of the dairy case, looking at the milk. Skim, two percent, whole milk, the ones with the red caps and the blue caps... and I just stood there. I stood there for I don't know how long, just staring at it, before I could finally make my arm reach out and pick up the one I came for. Richard was waiting in the car, listening to his talk radio, and he actually came inside to look for me because I was taking so long. I wasn't restless or fidgety or anything like that, no. I just couldn't seem to make myself move at a normal speed anymore. Everything is just so slow and takes so much effort.

I was sitting at the kitchen table this morning, just looking out the window with my coffee getting cold, and I thought, well, maybe I should just cancel. I don't like to be a bother to anyone, you know? I've never been one to run to the doctor for every little thing. I've always just pushed through. But... well, things have just been feeling so heavy lately. \n\nI've just been in this sort of rut, I guess you could call it. Just sitting in my chair, not really doing much of anything, watching the clock. I just keep thinking... well, I keep thinking it would be such a relief if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. \n\nIt's just this overwhelming feeling, like everything is just too much to carry anymore. I have this little corner set up in the sunroom where I do my watercolors. Oh, we had such a good time, just moving and laughing to the music. And I'd go walking with the ladies in the neighborhood, too. We had a little group, three or four of us depending on the day, and we'd walk the whole loop around the park, rain or shine, just talking about our families and the neighborhood. \n\nBut lately... I just don't know what's happened to me. I just leave my painting things sitting right there on the table. The water cup is bone dry and the brushes are probably getting stiff because I didn't even put them away properly the last time I touched them. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up in a panic or anything like that. \n\nAnd in the morning, I wake up at my usual time, right around six-thirty when the sun starts coming through the blinds in the bedroom. I suppose I should be thankful for that, at least, since I hear so many people my age complaining about insomnia and having to take those little pills to get any rest. But for me, the sleep itself is just the same as it's always been.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I would say that's exactly right. Most of the day, nearly every day. It's just... well, it starts right when I wake up. Like I said about waking up at six-thirty, when the sun comes through the blinds? Well, I open my eyes, and almost immediately, before I even sit up or put my feet on the floor, there's just this... this awful, heavy sadness right here in my chest. \n\nIt feels almost like a physical weight, like someone set a cinder block right on top of my ribs while I was sleeping. And I just lie there under the covers, and I feel like I've already failed the day before it even starts. It's the strangest thing. I haven't done anything yet, I haven't even made my coffee or brushed my teeth, and already I feel this overwhelming sense of... of defeat, I suppose. \n\nAnd it just stays with me. It doesn't lift. I used to be able to shake off a bad mood, you know? If I woke up on the wrong side of the bed, I'd go make myself a nice breakfast, maybe put on some music—I love the old Motown records, they always used to get my toes tapping—and by the time I was washing the dishes, I'd be feeling right as rain. But not anymore. Now, I carry that heavy sadness around with me all day long. \n\nI'll be sitting in my chair in the living room at two in the afternoon, looking out at the street, watching the neighbor's kids riding their bikes up and down the sidewalk, and I just feel so incredibly down. It's like a dark cloud that just follows me from room to room, from the minute I wake up with that heavy feeling in my chest until I finally go back to sleep. So yes, doctor, to answer your question, it really is most of the day, and it feels like it's been every single day for a while now. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even when you haven't done much of anything?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. It's exactly like you said—even when I haven't done a single blessed thing. It goes right back to what I was just telling you about my sleep, you know? Because I get a full night in bed, I really do. I sleep all the way through from the news until the sun comes up, but... well, when I finally do push the covers off and try to get moving, my body just feels completely worn out. It makes no sense to me, because I haven't done anything to be tired from! \n\nI used to have energy to spare, but now... well, just take the laundry, for example. I have my washer and dryer right there in a little closet off the kitchen, so it's not like I'm lugging baskets up and down two flights of steep basement stairs like we had to do in our old house on Elm Street. But just picking up that little plastic laundry basket—the white one with the blue handles—and carrying it those few steps into the kitchen... it just takes so much more out of me than it ever should. I'll set it down and have to lean against the counter or sit at the kitchen table just to catch my breath. \n\nAnd breakfast! Making breakfast used to be just second nature. I've made breakfast every day of my adult life. I'd whip up some eggs, maybe toast a couple slices of rye bread, get the coffee perking... but now, just standing at the stove or trying to put a simple meal together, it just absolutely exhausts me. I have to drag my feet across the linoleum. I feel like I've been doing hard labor before I've even poured my morning coffee. It's just this bone-deep tiredness, doctor, where every little movement feels like wading through molasses, and it stays with me all day long, even when I'm just sitting in my chair doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I sit in my chair by the front window—the one with the blue floral pattern, I've had it since about 1998, bought it at Sears back when they were still at the mall—and I can sit there and find every single word on the page without losing my place or getting distracted. And making decisions? I don't have too many big decisions to make these days, being on my own and not working, but I still handle all my own affairs perfectly well. I pay all my bills on time, I balance my checkbook down to the penny every Tuesday morning. I sit right at the kitchen table with my calculator, the one with the big numbers so I don't have to squint, and I go through all the receipts from the grocery store. Even the complicated articles about the city council meetings and the zoning laws for that new shopping plaza they want to build over on Route 9. It just... stays put, right where it's supposed to be. So, no, whether it's simple tasks like I mentioned before with the calculator, or harder ones like following the local politics in the paper, my concentration is just as good as it was thirty years ago. It's just the awful tiredness and that heavy sadness, like I told you. But the thinking part? No, I haven't found that hard at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite has been just about the same as it always is. I mean, I know I was telling you earlier about how exhausting it is just to stand at the stove and make breakfast, but I still eat it. I sit right down at that kitchen table—the one with the little yellow checkered tablecloth I bought at Kmart years ago—and I eat my eggs and my rye toast. \n\nI haven't noticed my clothes fitting any differently, either. I still wear the same slacks I've been wearing for the last five years, those comfortable ones with the elastic waistband. They aren't falling off me, and they aren't pinching me at the waist, so I must be staying right around the same weight. I don't step on the scale very often, to be perfectly honest with you. I have one of those old dial scales in the bathroom, tucked right between the sink and the bathtub, but half the time I forget it's even there. \n\nBut no, I make sure I eat my three meals a day. I might just have a sandwich for lunch—usually turkey with a little bit of mayonnaise on white bread, maybe a pickle on the side if I have a jar open in the fridge—and then something simple for dinner. I don't have the energy to cook big elaborate meals like I used to when the house was full, like roasting a whole chicken or making a big pot of beef stew that takes hours to simmer, but I still eat a normal amount. So no, my appetite and my weight, they've been perfectly steady. No changes there at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore—not even things you used to enjoy, like your watercolors or music?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. It's funny you mention the watercolors, because I was just looking at them the other day. I have this little setup in the spare room—the one that used to be my daughter's room before she moved out to Ohio, it gets that wonderful morning light through the east-facing window. I've had my easel set up there for, oh, it must be five or six years now. I used to go in there every Tuesday and Thursday morning after breakfast, mix my paints on that little plastic palette I bought at the craft store over on Main Street, and just paint for hours. I used to go to this senior dance class at the community center on Wednesday evenings. We'd do a little line dancing, some gentle ballroom steps—nothing too strenuous, just nice music and good company. I haven't been in weeks. The thought of putting on my shoes and driving over there just seems so pointless now. \n\nEven my morning walks with the ladies... I used to meet Shirley and Barbara at the park entrance right at 8:00 AM sharp. We'd walk the perimeter path, the one that goes around the duck pond, and chat about everything under the sun. Now, when the phone rings and I know it's Shirley calling to see if I'm coming, I just let it ring. I sit in my chair and listen to it ring, and I don't feel a single thing. I just leave all my things sitting there—my walking shoes by the door, my paints in the spare room—and I just can't find a single reason to pick them up. It's like all the color has just drained right out of everything I used to love.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—like nothing brings you pleasure and you're carrying this heavy sadness most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. She moved into the downstairs bedroom—the one we used to use as a den, where my husband had his old recliner and the television set we bought when they finally switched everything over to digital. We had to move all of his things out into the garage to make room for her bed and her dresser. \n\nShe's my mother's sister, Aunt Clara, and she's getting up there in years, you know, she just turned eighty-seven in October. She couldn't manage the stairs in her old house anymore, the one over on Elm Street with the big wrap-around porch. Beautiful house, but just too much for her to keep up with, especially after she had that little fall in the kitchen. So, we brought her here, and ever since then... well, that's when this whole heavy feeling really settled in. \n\nI remember it was just after we got her all unpacked. I had put her favorite floral quilt on the bed, and I just sat down in the living room and felt this awful, crushing weight come over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. Not one bit. I have my washer and dryer right there in a little closet off the kitchen, so it's not like I'm lugging baskets up and down two flights of steep basement stairs like we had to do in our old house on Elm Street. But just picking up that little plastic laundry basket—the white one with the blue handles—and carrying it those few steps into the kitchen... it just takes so much more out of me than it ever should. I'll set it down and have to lean against the counter or sit at the kitchen table just to catch my breath. \n\nAnd breakfast! Making breakfast used to be just second nature. I've made breakfast every day of my adult life. I'd whip up some eggs, maybe toast a couple slices of rye bread, get the coffee perking... but now, just standing at the stove or trying to put a simple meal together, it just absolutely exhausts me. I have to drag my feet across the linoleum. I feel like I've been doing hard labor before I've even poured my morning coffee. It's just this bone-deep tiredness, doctor, where every little movement feels like wading through molasses, and it stays with me all day long, even when I'm just sitting in my chair doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. My mind is actually... well, it's sharp as a tack, really. I mean, my body is exhausted, like I was just telling you with the laundry and the breakfast and all that, but my head? No, my thinking is just fine. \n\nI still do my word searches every single afternoon. I sit in my chair by the front window—the one with the blue floral pattern, I've had it since about 1998, bought it at Sears back when they were still at the mall—and I can sit there and find every single word on the page without losing my place or getting distracted. And making decisions? I don't have too many big decisions to make these days, being on my own and not working, but I still handle all my own affairs perfectly well. I pay all my bills on time, I balance my checkbook down to the penny every Tuesday morning. I sit right at the kitchen table with my calculator, the one with the big numbers so I don't have to squint, and I go through all the receipts from the grocery store. I don't get confused at all. \n\nI know some ladies my age start getting a little... well, a little foggy, you know? Forgetting where they put their pocketbooks or losing the plot of their television shows. My friend Martha, bless her heart, she can't even follow Jeopardy! anymore because it moves too fast for her, and she gets all turned around. But me? No, I can follow along just fine. I know exactly what's going on. I might be terribly sad, and I might be tired down to my bones, but my thinking is as clear as it's ever been. It's just... I just don't have the energy to actually *do* the things I'm thinking about, if you see what I mean. But the focusing part itself, no, I haven't had any trouble with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, doctor, no. I really don't have any trouble with my thinking or my concentration at all. It's a bit funny you ask it again, because I know I just went on and on about my checkbook and my word searches, but it really is the truth! My mind is perfectly fine. \n\nI can sit down with the Sunday paper—you know, the big thick one that gets delivered by that nice boy from down the street, Tommy, I think his name is, though he must be getting ready to graduate high school soon because he's gotten so tall—anyway, I can sit with that paper and read every single article on the front page, right through to the end, without losing my train of thought even once. Even the complicated articles about the city council meetings and the zoning laws for that new shopping plaza they want to build over on Route 9. I read all of it, and I understand it just fine. \n\nI don't have to go back and re-read paragraphs like some folks do when their minds start to wander. My mind doesn't wander at all. It just... stays put, right where it's supposed to be. So, no, whether it's simple tasks like I mentioned before with the calculator, or harder ones like following the local politics in the paper, my concentration is just as good as it was thirty years ago. It's just the awful tiredness and that heavy sadness, like I told you. But the thinking part? No, I haven't found that hard at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite has been just about the same as it always is. I mean, I know I was telling you earlier about how exhausting it is just to stand at the stove and make breakfast, but I still eat it. I sit right down at that kitchen table—the one with the little yellow checkered tablecloth I bought at Kmart years ago—and I eat my eggs and my rye toast. \n\nI haven't noticed my clothes fitting any differently, either. I still wear the same slacks I've been wearing for the last five years, those comfortable ones with the elastic waistband. They aren't falling off me, and they aren't pinching me at the waist, so I must be staying right around the same weight. I don't step on the scale very often, to be perfectly honest with you. I have one of those old dial scales in the bathroom, tucked right between the sink and the bathtub, but half the time I forget it's even there. \n\nBut no, I make sure I eat my three meals a day. I might just have a sandwich for lunch—usually turkey with a little bit of mayonnaise on white bread, maybe a pickle on the side if I have a jar open in the fridge—and then something simple for dinner. I don't have the energy to cook big elaborate meals like I used to when the house was full, like roasting a whole chicken or making a big pot of beef stew that takes hours to simmer, but I still eat a normal amount. So no, my appetite and my weight, they've been perfectly steady. No changes there at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore—not even things you used to enjoy, like your watercolors or music?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. It's funny you mention the watercolors, because I was just looking at them the other day. I have this little setup in the spare room—the one that used to be my daughter's room before she moved out to Ohio, it gets that wonderful morning light through the east-facing window. I've had my easel set up there for, oh, it must be five or six years now. I used to go in there every Tuesday and Thursday morning after breakfast, mix my paints on that little plastic palette I bought at the craft store over on Main Street, and just paint for hours. Flowers, mostly. Irises were my favorite. \n\nBut lately? I walk past that room, I look at the easel, I see the paints just sitting there getting dusty, and I just... I don't care. I can't make myself care. The feeling I used to get, that little spark of excitement when I'd get the color just right? It's completely gone. And it's not just the painting, either. I used to go to this senior dance class at the community center on Wednesday evenings. We'd do a little line dancing, some gentle ballroom steps—nothing too strenuous, just nice music and good company. I haven't been in weeks. The thought of putting on my shoes and driving over there just seems so pointless now. \n\nEven my morning walks with the ladies... I used to meet Shirley and Barbara at the park entrance right at 8:00 AM sharp. We'd walk the perimeter path, the one that goes around the duck pond, and chat about everything under the sun. Now, when the phone rings and I know it's Shirley calling to see if I'm coming, I just let it ring. I sit in my chair and listen to it ring, and I don't feel a single thing. I just leave all my things sitting there—my walking shoes by the door, my paints in the spare room—and I just can't find a single reason to pick them up. It's like all the color has just drained right out of everything I used to love.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—like nothing brings you pleasure and you're carrying this heavy sadness most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I'd say it's been going on for about four months. Yes, four months exactly, because it started right around the time my aunt came to live with us. She moved into the downstairs bedroom—the one we used to use as a den, where my husband had his old recliner and the television set we bought when they finally switched everything over to digital. We had to move all of his things out into the garage to make room for her bed and her dresser. \n\nShe's my mother's sister, Aunt Clara, and she's getting up there in years, you know, she just turned eighty-seven in October. She couldn't manage the stairs in her old house anymore, the one over on Elm Street with the big wrap-around porch. Beautiful house, but just too much for her to keep up with, especially after she had that little fall in the kitchen. So, we brought her here, and ever since then... well, that's when this whole heavy feeling really settled in. \n\nI remember it was just after we got her all unpacked. I had put her favorite floral quilt on the bed, and I just sat down in the living room and felt this awful, crushing weight come over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. Not one bit. Every single day for the last four months, I wake up and that heavy sadness is just sitting right there on my chest, before I even put my feet on the floor. It's been four long months of just... nothing. No joy in anything at all, just like I was saying about my paints and my walks. Just four months of this awful, heavy grayness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or that you can’t sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband, Richard—he's always been the observant one, you know, notices every little thing, like if I move a vase two inches to the left on the mantle—he's been saying something about that. He tells me I'm moving like everything takes extra effort now. Like I'm wading through molasses or something. And I suppose he's right. I just feel so heavy. Like when I go to get up from the sofa—the blue one we bought at Sears years ago, the one with the little matching throw pillows—it just takes me so much longer to actually get to my feet. I have to think about it, push myself up, and it just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd it's not just around the house, either. Even when I manage to get myself to the grocery store—the Stop & Shop over on County Road, I've been going there since they opened in '98—I'll just be standing there in the aisle. I went in last week just to get a carton of milk and some of those little oyster crackers for Aunt Clara's soup. And I just stood there in front of the dairy case, looking at the milk. Skim, two percent, whole milk, the ones with the red caps and the blue caps... and I just stood there. I stood there for I don't know how long, just staring at it, before I could finally make my arm reach out and pick up the one I came for. Richard was waiting in the car, listening to his talk radio, and he actually came inside to look for me because I was taking so long. I wasn't restless or fidgety or anything like that, no. I just couldn't seem to make myself move at a normal speed anymore. Everything is just so slow and takes so much effort.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, irritable... I wouldn't say irritable, exactly, though Richard might tell you differently if you asked him. But the slowness... yes, and just the sheer exhaustion of it all. It's like I have no energy left for anything, no matter what I do. 

I'll go to bed at a reasonable hour, you know, right after the ten o'clock news finishes. We always watch the local broadcast on channel four, the one with that nice meteorologist, Tom, who always has the lovely ties. And I'll lay there, and I might even sleep through the whole night, right until the sun comes up and hits the blinds in our room. A full night in bed, eight or nine hours sometimes. But when I wake up? I feel like I haven't slept a wink. My whole body just feels completely worn out, right down to my bones. 

And you mentioned small tasks taking effort... oh, it's just ridiculous now. It used to be I could have breakfast made, the kitchen wiped down, and a load of laundry going before nine in the morning. Now? Just the thought of making breakfast feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I'll stand there looking at the toaster—it's one of those nice four-slice ones we got for our anniversary a few years back, stainless steel—and I just feel so drained. Just pushing down the little lever for the toast feels like it takes more out of me than it should. 

And laundry... well, the laundry room is just off the kitchen, right next to the pantry where I keep all my canned goods and baking supplies. I used to just scoop up the basket from the bedroom, carry it down the hall, sort the lights and darks, and have it washing in no time. But yesterday, I picked up the basket—and it wasn't even full, mind you, just some towels and a few of Richard's shirts—and I only made it halfway down the hall before I had to set it down. My arms felt like lead. My whole body just felt so heavy and weak. I had to lean against the wall by the linen closet just to catch my breath. It's just this awful, deep fatigue that never goes away, no matter how much I rest. Everything just takes so much more out of me than it ever used to.

I've had my easel set up there for, oh, it must be five or six years now. Like when I go to get up from the sofa—the blue one we bought at Sears years ago, the one with the little matching throw pillows—it just takes me so much longer to actually get to my feet. Skim, two percent, whole milk, the ones with the red caps and the blue caps... and I just stood there. I stood there for I don't know how long, just staring at it, before I could finally make my arm reach out and pick up the one I came for. Richard was waiting in the car, listening to his talk radio, and he actually came inside to look for me because I was taking so long. I wasn't restless or fidgety or anything like that, no. I just couldn't seem to make myself move at a normal speed anymore. Everything is just so slow and takes so much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in the way you feel or move—like feeling so slowed down that even small tasks take a lot of effort, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, irritable... Yes, I would say that's exactly right. Most of the day, nearly every day. It's just... well, it starts right when I wake up. Like I said about waking up at six-thirty, when the sun comes through the blinds? Well, I open my eyes, and almost immediately, before I even sit up or put my feet on the floor, there's just this... this awful, heavy sadness right here in my chest. \n\nIt feels almost like a physical weight, like someone set a cinder block right on top of my ribs while I was sleeping. And I just lie there under the covers, and I feel like I've already failed the day before it even starts. It's the strangest thing. I haven't done anything yet, I haven't even made my coffee or brushed my teeth, and already I feel this overwhelming sense of... of defeat, I suppose. \n\nAnd it just stays with me. It doesn't lift. I used to be able to shake off a bad mood, you know? If I woke up on the wrong side of the bed, I'd go make myself a nice breakfast, maybe put on some music—I love the old Motown records, they always used to get my toes tapping—and by the time I was washing the dishes, I'd be feeling right as rain. But not anymore. Now, I carry that heavy sadness around with me all day long. \n\nI'll be sitting in my chair in the living room at two in the afternoon, looking out at the street, watching the neighbor's kids riding their bikes up and down the sidewalk, and I just feel so incredibly down. It's like a dark cloud that just follows me from room to room, from the minute I wake up with that heavy feeling in my chest until I finally go back to sleep. So yes, doctor, to answer your question, it really is most of the day, and it feels like it's been every single day for a while now. I just can't seem to shake it, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even when you haven't done much of anything?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens, yes. It's exactly like you said—even when I haven't done a single blessed thing. It goes right back to what I was just telling you about my sleep, you know? Because I get a full night in bed, I really do. I sleep all the way through from the news until the sun comes up, but... well, when I finally do push the covers off and try to get moving, my body just feels completely worn out. It makes no sense to me, because I haven't done anything to be tired from! \n\nI used to have energy to spare, but now... well, just take the laundry, for example. I have my washer and dryer right there in a little closet off the kitchen, so it's not like I'm lugging baskets up and down two flights of steep basement stairs like we had to do in our old house on Elm Street. But just picking up that little plastic laundry basket—the white one with the blue handles—and carrying it those few steps into the kitchen... it just takes so much more out of me than it ever should. I'll set it down and have to lean against the counter or sit at the kitchen table just to catch my breath. \n\nAnd breakfast! Making breakfast used to be just second nature. I've made breakfast every day of my adult life. I'd whip up some eggs, maybe toast a couple slices of rye bread, get the coffee perking... but now, just standing at the stove or trying to put a simple meal together, it just absolutely exhausts me. I have to drag my feet across the linoleum. I feel like I've been doing hard labor before I've even poured my morning coffee. It's just this bone-deep tiredness, doctor, where every little movement feels like wading through molasses, and it stays with me all day long, even when I'm just sitting in my chair doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. My mind is actually... well, it's sharp as a tack, really. I mean, my body is exhausted, like I was just telling you with the laundry and the breakfast and all that, but my head? No, my thinking is just fine. \n\nI still do my word searches every single afternoon. I sit in my chair by the front window—the one with the blue floral pattern, I've had it since about 1998, bought it at Sears back when they were still at the mall—and I can sit there and find every single word on the page without losing my place or getting distracted. And making decisions? I don't have too many big decisions to make these days, being on my own and not working, but I still handle all my own affairs perfectly well. I pay all my bills on time, I balance my checkbook down to the penny every Tuesday morning. I sit right at the kitchen table with my calculator, the one with the big numbers so I don't have to squint, and I go through all the receipts from the grocery store. I don't get confused at all. \n\nI know some ladies my age start getting a little... well, a little foggy, you know? Forgetting where they put their pocketbooks or losing the plot of their television shows. My friend Martha, bless her heart, she can't even follow Jeopardy! anymore because it moves too fast for her, and she gets all turned around. But me? No, I can follow along just fine. I know exactly what's going on. I might be terribly sad, and I might be tired down to my bones, but my thinking is as clear as it's ever been. It's just... I just don't have the energy to actually *do* the things I'm thinking about, if you see what I mean. But the focusing part itself, no, I haven't had any trouble with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately, even simple ones?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying a moment ago, doctor, no. I really don't have any trouble with my thinking or my concentration at all. It's a bit funny you ask it again, because I know I just went on and on about my checkbook and my word searches, but it really is the truth! My mind is perfectly fine. \n\nI can sit down with the Sunday paper—you know, the big thick one that gets delivered by that nice boy from down the street, Tommy, I think his name is, though he must be getting ready to graduate high school soon because he's gotten so tall—anyway, I can sit with that paper and read every single article on the front page, right through to the end, without losing my train of thought even once. Even the complicated articles about the city council meetings and the zoning laws for that new shopping plaza they want to build over on Route 9. I read all of it, and I understand it just fine. \n\nI don't have to go back and re-read paragraphs like some folks do when their minds start to wander. My mind doesn't wander at all. It just... stays put, right where it's supposed to be. So, no, whether it's simple tasks like I mentioned before with the calculator, or harder ones like following the local politics in the paper, my concentration is just as good as it was thirty years ago. It's just the awful tiredness and that heavy sadness, like I told you. But the thinking part? No, I haven't found that hard at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite has been just about the same as it always is. I mean, I know I was telling you earlier about how exhausting it is just to stand at the stove and make breakfast, but I still eat it. I sit right down at that kitchen table—the one with the little yellow checkered tablecloth I bought at Kmart years ago—and I eat my eggs and my rye toast. \n\nI haven't noticed my clothes fitting any differently, either. I still wear the same slacks I've been wearing for the last five years, those comfortable ones with the elastic waistband. They aren't falling off me, and they aren't pinching me at the waist, so I must be staying right around the same weight. I don't step on the scale very often, to be perfectly honest with you. I have one of those old dial scales in the bathroom, tucked right between the sink and the bathtub, but half the time I forget it's even there. \n\nBut no, I make sure I eat my three meals a day. I might just have a sandwich for lunch—usually turkey with a little bit of mayonnaise on white bread, maybe a pickle on the side if I have a jar open in the fridge—and then something simple for dinner. I don't have the energy to cook big elaborate meals like I used to when the house was full, like roasting a whole chicken or making a big pot of beef stew that takes hours to simmer, but I still eat a normal amount. So no, my appetite and my weight, they've been perfectly steady. No changes there at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore—not even things you used to enjoy, like your watercolors or music?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. Yes, I suppose that's exactly it. It's funny you mention the watercolors, because I was just looking at them the other day. I have this little setup in the spare room—the one that used to be my daughter's room before she moved out to Ohio, it gets that wonderful morning light through the east-facing window. I've had my easel set up there for, oh, it must be five or six years now. I used to go in there every Tuesday and Thursday morning after breakfast, mix my paints on that little plastic palette I bought at the craft store over on Main Street, and just paint for hours. Flowers, mostly. Irises were my favorite. \n\nBut lately? I walk past that room, I look at the easel, I see the paints just sitting there getting dusty, and I just... I don't care. I can't make myself care. The feeling I used to get, that little spark of excitement when I'd get the color just right? It's completely gone. And it's not just the painting, either. I used to go to this senior dance class at the community center on Wednesday evenings. We'd do a little line dancing, some gentle ballroom steps—nothing too strenuous, just nice music and good company. I haven't been in weeks. The thought of putting on my shoes and driving over there just seems so pointless now. \n\nEven my morning walks with the ladies... I used to meet Shirley and Barbara at the park entrance right at 8:00 AM sharp. We'd walk the perimeter path, the one that goes around the duck pond, and chat about everything under the sun. Now, when the phone rings and I know it's Shirley calling to see if I'm coming, I just let it ring. I sit in my chair and listen to it ring, and I don't feel a single thing. I just leave all my things sitting there—my walking shoes by the door, my paints in the spare room—and I just can't find a single reason to pick them up. It's like all the color has just drained right out of everything I used to love.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—like nothing brings you pleasure and you're carrying this heavy sadness most of the day?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been quite a while now. I'd say it's been going on for about four months. Yes, four months exactly, because it started right around the time my aunt came to live with us. She moved into the downstairs bedroom—the one we used to use as a den, where my husband had his old recliner and the television set we bought when they finally switched everything over to digital. We had to move all of his things out into the garage to make room for her bed and her dresser. \n\nShe's my mother's sister, Aunt Clara, and she's getting up there in years, you know, she just turned eighty-seven in October. She couldn't manage the stairs in her old house anymore, the one over on Elm Street with the big wrap-around porch. Beautiful house, but just too much for her to keep up with, especially after she had that little fall in the kitchen. So, we brought her here, and ever since then... well, that's when this whole heavy feeling really settled in. \n\nI remember it was just after we got her all unpacked. I had put her favorite floral quilt on the bed, and I just sat down in the living room and felt this awful, crushing weight come over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. Not one bit. Every single day for the last four months, I wake up and that heavy sadness is just sitting right there on my chest, before I even put my feet on the floor. It's been four long months of just... nothing. No joy in anything at all, just like I was saying about my paints and my walks. Just four months of this awful, heavy grayness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or that you can’t sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Well, my husband, Richard—he's always been the observant one, you know, notices every little thing, like if I move a vase two inches to the left on the mantle—he's been saying something about that. He tells me I'm moving like everything takes extra effort now. Like I'm wading through molasses or something. And I suppose he's right. I just feel so heavy. Like when I go to get up from the sofa—the blue one we bought at Sears years ago, the one with the little matching throw pillows—it just takes me so much longer to actually get to my feet. I have to think about it, push myself up, and it just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd it's not just around the house, either. Even when I manage to get myself to the grocery store—the Stop & Shop over on County Road, I've been going there since they opened in '98—I'll just be standing there in the aisle. I went in last week just to get a carton of milk and some of those little oyster crackers for Aunt Clara's soup. And I just stood there in front of the dairy case, looking at the milk. Skim, two percent, whole milk, the ones with the red caps and the blue caps... and I just stood there. I stood there for I don't know how long, just staring at it, before I could finally make my arm reach out and pick up the one I came for. Richard was waiting in the car, listening to his talk radio, and he actually came inside to look for me because I was taking so long. I wasn't restless or fidgety or anything like that, no. I just couldn't seem to make myself move at a normal speed anymore. Everything is just so slow and takes so much effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in the way you feel or move—like feeling so slowed down that even small tasks take a lot of effort, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, irritable... I wouldn't say irritable, exactly, though Richard might tell you differently if you asked him. But the slowness... yes, and just the sheer exhaustion of it all. It's like I have no energy left for anything, no matter what I do. \n\nI'll go to bed at a reasonable hour, you know, right after the ten o'clock news finishes. We always watch the local broadcast on channel four, the one with that nice meteorologist, Tom, who always has the lovely ties. And I'll lay there, and I might even sleep through the whole night, right until the sun comes up and hits the blinds in our room. A full night in bed, eight or nine hours sometimes. But when I wake up? I feel like I haven't slept a wink. My whole body just feels completely worn out, right down to my bones. \n\nAnd you mentioned small tasks taking effort... oh, it's just ridiculous now. It used to be I could have breakfast made, the kitchen wiped down, and a load of laundry going before nine in the morning. Now? Just the thought of making breakfast feels like I'm being asked to climb a mountain. I'll stand there looking at the toaster—it's one of those nice four-slice ones we got for our anniversary a few years back, stainless steel—and I just feel so drained. Just pushing down the little lever for the toast feels like it takes more out of me than it should. \n\nAnd laundry... well, the laundry room is just off the kitchen, right next to the pantry where I keep all my canned goods and baking supplies. I used to just scoop up the basket from the bedroom, carry it down the hall, sort the lights and darks, and have it washing in no time. But yesterday, I picked up the basket—and it wasn't even full, mind you, just some towels and a few of Richard's shirts—and I only made it halfway down the hall before I had to set it down. My arms felt like lead. My whole body just felt so heavy and weak. I had to lean against the wall by the linen closet just to catch my breath. It's just this awful, deep fatigue that never goes away, no matter how much I rest. Everything just takes so much more out of me than it ever used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my thoughts... I suppose they do feel a bit slower, but not... not in a way that I'd say it's hard to think, exactly. It's more like... well, let me explain. You see, I've always been the sort of person who could keep track of a dozen things at once. I used to be able to remember the grocery list, Richard's doctor appointments, when the car needed its oil changed, and what time Aunt Clara needed her heart medication, all without writing a single thing down. I kept it all right up here, organized like a filing cabinet. 

But lately... well, it's just that my mind feels a little clouded, I guess you could say. Like when I try to read the Sunday paper. We get the Times delivered, the boy on the bicycle drops it at the end of the driveway every Sunday morning right around six-thirty, rain or shine. He's such a good boy, always wraps it in plastic if it looks like it might drizzle. Anyway, I used to sit down with a cup of coffee—Maxwell House, I've always preferred the blue can—and read the front section cover to cover. Now, I'll read a paragraph, and by the time I get to the end of it, I have to go back and read it again because it just didn't sink in. 

It's not that I can't understand the words, or that my brain is moving at a snail's pace, but it's just... foggy. Like trying to look through a dirty window. I'll be in the middle of a thought, like figuring out what to make for dinner—maybe a nice pot roast with carrots and those little red potatoes Richard likes—and then the thought just sort of drifts away from me. I wouldn't say it's hard to think at my usual speed, no, I just... I don't seem to hold onto the thoughts the way I used to. But I don't feel like my brain is moving in slow motion, if that's what you mean. Just a bit muddled, perhaps.

And I just lie there under the covers, and I feel like I've already failed the day before it even starts. It's the strangest thing. But not anymore. Now, I carry that heavy sadness around with me all day long. \n\nI'll be sitting in my chair in the living room at two in the afternoon, looking out at the street, watching the neighbor's kids riding their bikes up and down the sidewalk, and I just feel so incredibly down. It's like a dark cloud that just follows me from room to room, from the minute I wake up with that heavy feeling in my chest until I finally go back to sleep. It's just this bone-deep tiredness, doctor, where every little movement feels like wading through molasses, and it stays with me all day long, even when I'm just sitting in my chair doing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. No, I haven't found that hard at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating much less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't have the energy to cook big elaborate meals like I used to when the house was full, like roasting a whole chicken or making a big pot of beef stew that takes hours to simmer, but I still eat a normal amount. So no, my appetite and my weight, they've been perfectly steady. No changes there at all.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in everything, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore—not even things you used to enjoy, like your watercolors or music?\n\nPatient: Well... yes. I'd say it's been going on for about four months. Yes, four months exactly, because it started right around the time my aunt came to live with us. We had to move all of his things out into the garage to make room for her bed and her dresser. \n\nShe's my mother's sister, Aunt Clara, and she's getting up there in years, you know, she just turned eighty-seven in October. She couldn't manage the stairs in her old house anymore, the one over on Elm Street with the big wrap-around porch. I had put her favorite floral quilt on the bed, and I just sat down in the living room and felt this awful, crushing weight come over me, and it just hasn't lifted since. Not one bit.
